[trialAccount t17960113-91]
148. PAULO OFFRACIUS was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 4th of January, a silver watch, value 40s. and one metal chain, value 6d. the property of Juan Chevrier.(The prosecutor and prisoner being foreigners, an interpreter and a Jury of half foreigners were sworn.
JURY.
Martin Robinson,
Henry Sace,
Solomon Erwood,
Jacob Richcart,
Francis Feather,
Godfrey Shore,
Joseph Simpson,
Peter Prestiot,
John Wilmot,
Guiseppe Shum,
Corneliud Paas,
Peter Poland.
JONATHAN MURRAY sworn.
I am a pawnbroker, in East Smithfield; the prisoner pledged a watch with me. Q. Did he speak English when he pledged it? - A. No.
JUAN CHEVRIER sworn.
I am a foreigner; I lodged on the 4th of January in the house of Joseph Gomez; it is No. 4; I don't know the name of the street.
Q. Do you know where the prisoner lived? - A. Yes; in the same house; I lost my watch on the 4th of January; I had left it locked up in my chest about twelve o'clock at noon; when I returned at seven in the evening, I missed it.
Q. Do you know who took it out of your chest? - A. Yes; the prisoner at the bar; it was a silver watch, and the chain a metal one; the prisoner with his own key opened it.
Q. Are you sure you did not give or lend him the watch? - A. No; I left it locked up in the chest.
Murray. On Monday the 24th of January, about four or five o'clock in the afternoon, Paulo Offracius, the prisoner, pawned a silver watch, for two guineas, which the prosecutor has got.
Q. How did you make him understand? - A. A few days before, he came with a countryman of his to pawn a pair of knee buckles; he understood English; and when he came to take out the knee-buckles, be pledged this watch; he asked three guineas and an half for it; he pledged it in the name of John Fracius; he could understand money in English; and he said, if I would give him the paper, he would come again for it; by order of the Magistrates, I gave it to the prosecutor; the maker of it lives but a few doors from us, Mr. Jackson, a man that frequently serves foreigners with watches.(The watch was produced in Court.)
Q. To Chevrier. Are you sure that is your property? - A. Yes.
Murray. This is the watch I took in pledge from the prisoner.
ELIZABETH GOMEZ sworn.
I am the wife of Joseph Gomez; the prosecutor and prisoner both lodged with me: On Monday the 4th of January, the prosecutor came home about seven in the evening; he went up to his chest, and came down, and said he had lost his watch; my mother and I went to a great many pawnbrokers, and found the watch at Mr. Murray's.
Q. Had you no conversation with the prisoner? A. None at all.
Q. What is your mother's name? - A. Ann Evans.
Murray. Mr. Beare came to enquire after the watch about seven in the evening; and about eight o'clock this young woman and her mother came upon the same business.
JOSEPH BEARE sworn.
I apprehended the prisoner; the prosecutor came to my house, and said he had been robbed of his watch; that is all I know of it.
Prisoner's defence. I pawned the watch with the prosecutor's leave; I had lent him two guineas; I asked him for the two guineas; he said he had not two guineas; I said, if he had not got it, I was in want of money, and he said pawn the watch; the prosecutor's chest was open, and he told me I might take it out.
Q. (To Chevrier.) Did you owe the prisoner two guineas? - A. I never knew him in my life.
Q. Did you owe him two guineas? - A. Not a farthing.
Q. Did you give him leave to pawn your watch? - A. No.
Q. (To Mrs. Gomez.) How long have you known the prisoner? - A. About three weeks.
Q. Do you know any thing of his character at all? - A. No; I have known the prosecutor about three months.
Q. Do you know of any transaction between these two people about money? - A. No.
Q. You never heard of his lending the prosecutor any money? - A. No.
Q. Were they upon any terms together? - A. No further than living in the house together.
Q. (To the prisoner.) Is there any body here that knows you? - A. No.
Q. Where is your captain? - A. At Jamaica.
GUILTY. (Aged 18.)(The prisoner was recommended to mercy by the Jury and the prosecutor.)
Tried before Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.
Court. (To Mrs. Gomez.) Q. Has he any thing at your house? - A. He has a chest and a large jacket.
He must have his things. - A. By all means; he came from Portsmouth when he came to me.
Q. (To Chevrier.) Do you know what ship he belongs to? - A. No.
Beare. He came over in the Ram.
Mrs. Gomez. He is a very sober, quiet man.
Beare. I would undertake to get him a ship, and I dare say he would behave well; he has been very ill, and is ill now; he was examined when he was at the Justice's office, he is very bad, and he said he had pawned the watch to pay the doctor.
(The Court ordered the Interpreter to inform the prisoner that he had been found guilty, but at the intercession of the prosecutor and the Jury, the Court intended to shew him mercy, and after a short imprisonment, he would be sent to the hospital to be cured).

149. WILLIAM SHELTON was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 25th of December, an ox-hide salted, value 20s. the property of Richard Gearing.
Second Count. Laying it to be the property of William Alston.(The case was opened by Mr. Gurney).
WILLIAM ALSTON sworn.
Examined by Mr. Gurney. I live in Long-lane, Southwark; I am a tanner, I delivered one, hundred and nine hides to be shipped on board a barge, marked T, directed to Charles Poole, tanner, Taunton.
Q. Did you deliver any others? - A. Yes, fifty-two, directed to Mr. Poole, Overshaw, near Bridgewater; they were marked on the inside of the hide, on the fleshy side; there was a piece of sacking tied on, with a tar rope, such as is made use of to tie up raw hides.
Q. To whom did you deliver them? - A. I delivered them to William Saunders; I saw them delivered.
Q. Who is Saunders? - A. I believe he is servant to Richard Gearing; he likewise gave me a receipt for them.
Q. Did you at any time afterwards see any hides that were delivered to him? - A. On the 29th, I was in Leadenhall market; Mr. Cork asked me if I had lost a hide, I said I did not know; he told me a hide was brought by a porter that morning, and desired he would sell it; Mr. Cork observed that it was a hide that was salted, and he supposed it had been stole or lost, and therefore he did not offer it for sale; he asked me if I had lost a hide.
Q. In consequence of this, did you go to the barge? - A. Not immediately.
Q. When you examined the hide, did you know it? - A. I knew it; I shipped the same on the 21st, in Saunders's barge, at Brook's Wharf, I went to the barge and found Mr. Saunders.
Q. Did you look over the hides? - A. No, I did not.
Q. Have you any doubt as to the hide? - A. No, I have not.
Q. Was it one of the fifty-two? - A. Yes; I marked it, as I seldom mark them; I bought fifty for Mr. Poole, of Overshaw; I marked them with P, that I might knew them, they are falted hides, to preserve them from putresaction.
Q. They are not saleable in this town? - A. No.
WILLIAM CORK sworn.
Examined by Mr. Gurney. I am a salesman in Leadenhall-market: On the 29th of December, a man, who was a stranger to me, throws down before my window, a hide, his name is Cranfield.
Court. Q. Was that the same hide you shewed to Mr. Alston? - A. On the person's putting it down, I said, my friend, how came you by this hide, I am afraid it is either stolen or lost; he said, a man brought it to a public-house, at the Saracen's-head, in Friday-street, where he was porter, and asked him to bring it to market to some salesman to sell.
Court. Q. Did he tell you who he was? - A. No; it remained on my premisses, and I enquired of three gentlemen, if they had lost any; upon enquiring further, a person told me he thought it was one of Mr. Alston's, and said, he would enquire, desiring me not to dispose of the hide.
Court. Q. Was the hide you shewed to Mr. Alston that which you received from Cranfield? - A. The hide I received from Cranfield, I shewed to Mr. Alston.
WILLIAM CRANFIELD sworn.
I am a porter, at the Saracen's-head, Friday-street; between the 21st and the 29th of December, the day I went to Mr. Cork, the prisoner came to me at the Saracen's-head, Friday-street, and told me he had found a hide in Thames-street, and asked me where he could sell it; I told him the only way would be to carry it to a salesman; he said, I should be glad if you will carry it for me, you have no occasion to be afraid to carry it for me, for I came honestly by it; I carried it in a bag; as soon as I put it out of the bag, Mr. Cork told me it was either lost or stole.
Court. Q. Are you sure that was the same hide you had from the prisoner? - A. Yes.
Mr. Gurney. Q. Was the prisoner to come to you after you came from Mr. Cork? - A. He did not come then, I saw him afterwards.
Q. Did you tell him what Mr. Cork said of its being lost or stolen? - A. Yes.
Q. When did you see the prisoner next? - A. Not till a week after that.
Q. He never came to take the money? - A. No.
Q. Did you know him before this? - A. Yes.
Court. Q. You knew the prisoner? - A. Yes; I am sure he is the man.
Jury. Q. He brought the hide in a sack? - A. In a bag.
WILLIAM SAUNDERS sworn.
Examined by Mr. Gurney. Q. I belong to Mr. Richard Gearing 's barge.
Q. Did you receive any hides from Mr. Alston? - A. One hundred and nine and fifty-two on the 1st of December.
Q. Did Mr. Alston come at any time afterwards? - A. Yes; he asked me, if I had all the hides; I looked, and found one was taken out; it was taken out of the fifty-two; only fifty-one remained.
Prisoner's defence. About half after five o'clock, I had done my work; I went to make water, and found this hide in Thames-street, and kept it till the Tuesday following; and then I went to this porter, at the Saracen's-head, to take it to market to sell it for me; he called at Mr. Cork's, in Leadenhall-market, and was stopped; I met a young man that worked in the deal way, and he told me, that two ships were to land at Gravesend; I went, and there was no such thing; I came back to Deptford as fast as I could; coming to London, I found a young man, he gave me work; and on Monday, about two o'clock, the constable came to take me.
Court. Q. Was there any body present, that saw you find it? - A. No; it was about six o'clock, on Christmas Eve.(The prisoner called two witnesses, who gave him a good character).
GUILTY. (Aged 33.)
Tried by the London Jury, before Mr. COMMON SERGEANT.
[Imprisonment. See summary.]
[Fine. See summary.]

150. WILLIAM MORRIS was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 1st of Novenber, a silver skewer, value 18s. and a silver table-spoon, value 10s. the property of Joseph Barnes.
JOSEPH BARNES sworn.
I live in Water-lane, Tower-street; I am a victualler: About six weeks ago, I missed a tablespoon and a silver skewer; I take an account, once a week, of the plate in my house; and I found these articles deficient: the prisoner used frequently to come to the house, with his master, Mr. Dickson, an attorney, in Mincing-lane; I employed Mr. Dickson, in whatever I had to do in the law line; the prisoner had access to my house; he used to go into the kitchen, where the plate and things were; I had not seen the prisoner for a considerable time, it might be a month or six weeks, when I missed these articles.
Q. Could you be a month missing these articles? - A. They could not have been taken till within a week, without my missing them; I missed them the latter end of November, or beginning of December.
Q. Where did you see this spoon again, and when? - A. On the 16th of January, in my own counting-house; Mr. Murl, partner with Mr. Cox, sent the spoon, in two pieces, and the skewer, in three; Mr. Bennet is here, who belongs to Mr. Murl.
Q. When did you last see him, before you missed the things? - A. In the course of that week.
Q. Then you now correct yourself? - A. Yes; if I said otherwise I was mistaken.
Q. When Mr. Murl produced them, was any body with him? - A. Yes; a clerk of Mr. Dickson's.
Cross-examined by Mr. Knapp. Q. This spoon and the skewer were lost, as you imagine, or, at least, might be lost out of your kitchen? - A. I only suppose so; I did not see them taken away.
Q. You keep a tavern in Water-lane, Tower-street? - A. Yes.
Q. Of course you have a good deal of company resort to your house? - A. Yes.
Q. And a vast number of persons have access to your kitchen, as well as Mr. Dickson and his servant? - A. Yes; of course.
Q. And, of course, in the way of your business, as a tavern-keeper, your plate must lie in that kitchen liable to the inroads of any body that thinks fit to come? - A. Yes.
Q. The prisoner had been in your house in the course of the week preceding the time of your missing them? - A. Yes.
Q. A great many persons might have been in your kitchen in the course of that week? - A. Yes.
Q. In the beginning of December, or the beginning of November, you missed them, after rumaging, and the property was not found till six weeks after? - A. No.
Q. Have you seen Mr. Murl, who brought the property to you to-day? - A. No.
Q. Have you seen the clerk who came with Mr. Murl, with the spoons and the skewer, to-day? - A. No; I have not.
JOSEPH BENNET sworn.
I live with Messrs. Cox, Murl, and Co. refiners, in Little Britain: On the 28th of November last, the prisoner at the bar brought a table-spoon and askewer to offer me for sale, at our counting-house, in Little Britain; the spoon was in two, and the skewer in three pieces; from their appearance, I thought there was something improper in the business, and I detained them.
Q. What passed between you and the prisoner respecting these articles? - A. I asked him, from whom he brought them; he said, it was nothing to me, if I did not like to buy them, I might give them him back; I told him, I should not, till I was more satisfied how he came by them; I then asked him, what his name was; he said, his name was Morris; I asked him, where he lived; he said, he lived with a Mr. Dickson, an attorney, in Mincing-lane; I then told him, if he brought a memorandum from Mr. Dickson, or brought Mr. Dickson with him, if our gentlemen were in the way, the property would be delivered up to him; he then went away, leaving the things in my possession, in a kind of huff, because I would not deliver him up the property; when Mr. Murl came back from dinner, the same evening, I informed him of the circumstance.
What passed between you and Mr. Murl you must not tell us. - A. The prisoner called again upon the 5th of January; the articles had laid upon a desk of mine from the 28th of November till the prisoner called again upon the 5th of January; there were three or four other clerks, besides myself, present; he told me, he had come for those articles he left the 28th of November; I told him, I could not deliver them up, without the authority of my master; if he would call the next morning, about eleven, they would be, one, or both, in the way; he said, he could not call at eleven, he would call between the hours of four and six in the evening, those being the hours of absence from office; I saw Mr. Murl about an hour and a half after this, and I communicated it to Mr. Murl; the next morning, the 6th, I delivered the two articles to Mr. Murl, in the same condition in which I had received them from the prisoner; the next time I saw them was about three or four hours after; when Mr. Murl came back from 'Change, he brought them back with him, to the counting-house, and I gave them, the next morning, to Mr. Cox, to take them to the Mansion-house; I went with him, to the Mansion-house; they were then left in the hands of the constable, who is here, his name is Juson.
Q. When was he apprehended? - A. On the 7th, by means of Mr. Barnes; I was sent for on the 7th, to attend the Lord Mayor.
Q. The day before he was not in custody? - A. Not that I know of.
Q. Who apprehended the prisoner? - A. I don't know.
Cross-examined by Mr. Knapp. Q. You are clerk to the house of Cox and Murl? - A. Yes.
Q. That is one of the most respectable houses in the city of London, as refiners? - A. None more so.
Q. The prisoner at the bar came with these things, and you told him, if he would bring a memorandum from Mr. Dickson, his master, you would purchase them at a future day? - A. Yes; if our gentlemen were satisfied, it was to be delivered up.
Q. The prisoner, who brought the spoon and the other thing broke, gave his own name? - A. He did.
Q. The very name by which he is indicted, where he lived, and who was his master? - A. He did.
Q. And though you had entertained suspicions, and would not deliver up the property, he came again to you? - A. Yes.
DANIEL JUSON sworn.
I am a constable of Tower-ward, (produces the property): I received it from one of my Lord Mayor's clerks, at the Mansion-house.
Q. These are the articles that were produced before the Lord Mayor? - A. Yes.
Q. (To Bennett). Look at these things, and see if they are the same? - A. I believe they are.
Q. Have you any doubt about it? - A. I have not; they have been out of my possession a long while.
Q. But where the articles you gave to Mr. Cox, the same things that were produced before the Lord Mayor? - A. They were.
Q. How many pieces are there there? - A. The spoon in two, the skewer in three; just as they were when the prisoner brought them to me; there is a mark of W S M upon the spoon, there has been a mark upon the skewer, which is erased out.
Q. (To Barnes). Look at these articles, and see if you can put them together? - A. They appear as if they belonged to each other; the broken parts fit each other; and it has the cypher upon it, W S M; I had had them five years.
Q. Can you undertake to swear that they are your's? - A. Yes; here is the fellow spoon to it.(producing it), with the same cypher; I have also the fellow to the skewer, (producing it); the cypher upon it is the same; and upon the broken one it is defaced.
Jury. The cypher and crest are both nearly out.
Prisoner. I leave my defence to my counsel.
For the prisoner.
ALEXANDER DICKSON sworn.
Examined by Mr. Knapp. Q. You are an attorney, in Mincing-lane? - A. Yes.
Q. Do you know the prisoner at the bar? - A. Yes; he lived with me about four years, up to the time he was taken; I have trusted him with several hundred pounds; I might say two thousand pounds, to go to the banker's; and I have left him in my house, with five thousand pounds property, and never missed a shilling.
Q. Is your opinion such, of him, that if he were discharged at this moment, you would take him again? - A. I would.
Q. As to his general character for honesty, have you any doubt of it? - A. I never heard any thing against him till this charge; or I would not have trusted him in my house.
Court. Q. What was he with you as clerk? - A. Not particularly as clerk; he used to go of messages, and errands.
Q. What age is he? - A. I take him to be about forty years.
Q. What salary had he? - A. He had no particular salary whatever; he used to go with me wherever I went almost.
Q. By what means did his subsistence arise? - A. When he wanted half-a-guinea I let him have it.
Q. Had he no demand upon you for his work and labour? - A. Never; I used to clothe him.
Q. Did he live in the house with you? - A. He frequently slept in the house; if I went any where he dined with me.
Q. He was under no regular establishment with you? - A. No.
Q. And his living with you was accidentally what you close to give him in return for such service as he did you? - A. Exactly so.
Q. Had he been bred up in the office of an attorney? - A. No; the way I first became acquainted with him was, I tried a cause for him, between him and the sheriffs of London, in which I succeeded, and recovered 300l. and odd for him; he was a china-man.
Q. When he came into your service, there was an end of his china trade? - A. Yes.
Q. How came you to think of taking this man, in any shape, into your service? - A. I took him out of charity; for all that he had to receive out of that business, he had to pay again directly.
Q. Has he lived with you ever since? - A. Yes: I have trusted him to take money to the banker's, and he never robbed me of a shilling; I never heard any thing bad of him in my life till the present time.
Q. Is he a single man? - A. Yes; he used to sleep in my dining-room.
JOHN CLEGHORN sworn.
I have declined business some years; I was a wholesale clothesman; I live in the Minories: I have known the prisoner, I should suppose, near twenty years; I never heard any thing disrespectful of him; I never heard of his being guilty of any thing like a felony; I never heard his honesty impeached before.
GUILTY.
(The Prisoner was recommended to mercy, by the Jury and Prosecutor, on account of his good character).
Tried by the London Jury, before Mr. Baron THOMPSON.
Court. (To Dickson). Q. Are you now willing to receive him again into your service? - A. I am.
Court. (To the Prisoner). You have stood in great need of that character; that has induced the prosecutor, and the Gentlemen of the Jury, to recommend you to mercy, upon this occasion; had it not been for that recommendation, and that character, which, I believe has come from a description of men, very different from those, whom unfortunately we see daily and hourly in this place, your punishment would have been very different; but there is no doubt, that the persons who have appeared in your favour are persons of character, and their evidence having impressed the mind of the Jury, has had its due weight. The Court have taken into consideration the circumstances of your case, and finding the readiness of Mr. Dixon to receive you again into his service, and to entertain you as he has been accustomed to do, are desirous that you should return to that employment, bearing in mind the lesson that you have had to day-that, till the moment before this offence, your character has not received any impeachment, you will be careful during the remainder of your life, and use double diligence that, you may keep it in future unspotted, remembering the narrow escape you have had; the sentence of this Court in consequence of that good character, is, that you be fined One Shilling, and discharged.

151. THOMAS CAVERNOR was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 12th of January, two pieces of brass, each of the weight of four pounds, value 2s. 6d. the property of George Penton the elder, and George Penton the younger.
GEORGE PENTON, junior, sworn.
I am in partnership with George Penton the elder, he is a first cousin of mine, I have no other partner; we carry on the business of brass-founders, in New-street, St. Bride's. I know nothing of the robbery myself.
- TILWORTH sworn.
I am servant to the two Mr. Pentons; I told Mr. Penton I suspected the prisoner of robbing him; he had been a labourer in the shop between two and three years, he worked in the loundry; I workedat turning the brass; I have lived with Mr. Penton seven years last Lord-Mayor's-day: On Tuesday the 12th of January, about a quarter before five o'clock in the afternoon, I discovered two piece of brass in a place where I had frequently observed the prisoner come from after dusk, it was in the necessary, hid among some cinders and dirt that were put there for washing; it was two pigs of brass, or rather one pig broke into two; upon finding them, informed M. Penton.
Q. Had you taken them out before you informed Mr. Penton? - A. I took one up in my hand and put it down again where I found it, and then I informed Mr. Penton of it; he desired me to go, with another man, of the name of Thomas Seares, and mark it, which we did in a few minutes after, with a punch; I have the punch that I marked one of the pieces with in my pocket, I marked it in two places at one end; we then put them back in the same manner, as near as we could, and covered them as they were before; then we each of us bored a hole through a thin partition in the next yard, which is Seares's yard; I left him in the yard listening, and came back again into the shop; when I saw the brass again, the prisoner had one piece, and Seares the other; that was about three quarters of an hour after we had marked them.
Q. How was it you came to look among the cinders? - A. I suspected the prisoner of hiding things there; when I saw the cinders the day before, they were quite flat; and when I saw them then, they were heaped up in the middle.
Prisoner. Did you see me lay the things in the necessary? - A. No.
THOMAS SEARES sworn.
I work for Messrs. Penton; the prisoner was employed by them. On the 12th of January, about five, or a few minutes before five o'clock, Mr. Penton desired me and Tilworth to go and mark some brass that was in the necessary, hid in some cinders from the furnace; Tilworth took out a pig broke in half, he marked one piece, with a small punch, in two places; and I marked the other piece in one place also, with a punch; we put them in the same place again, among the cinders; we returned to the shop where Tilworth works; and I got a gimblet and bored a hole in the wainscoat, and Tilworth bored another in my yard, which joins this place; he went into the shop to work, and I staid to watch in my own yard, till about twenty minutes, or a quarter before six o'clock; I heard a rustling among the cinders, it is a very thin partition, but I could not see any body, it was then dark; I looked through one of the holes, but there was no light on the other side; upon hearing the rustling among the cinders, I found that the person had got one out, because the rustling stopped; and in the space of half a minute, I heard it again; then I heard a person come out of the necessary into the passage that leads into the street; I then went along the passage of the house that I live in, they are parallel to one another, and at the end of the passage my street-door was open, and I saw the prisoner go by, and heard the gates shut, he went along the foot-pavement past me; I followed him about two doors, and spoke to him; I said to him, Thomas, where are you going; he said he was going to tea; I put my hand before his apron, and said, you have got that here that does not belong to you; he answered, no; I said, I was certain he had a pig of brass; he begged for me to let him go; he gave me a piece from under his apron; I told him I was sure he had more; his answer was, no; I rung the bell, and desired one of my shop-mates to send Tilworth down, and I sent him over to Mr. Penton's; in that time he gave me the other pig of brass, and said, he had never had more than half a dozen; then Mr. Penton came, and he was delivered to the constable, with the pieces of brass; he has had them ever since.
Q. In what part of Mr. Penton's premisses did this brass use to lie? - A. In the cellar of the house where the manufactory is carried on.
Q. Who had access to that cellar? - A. We always had to go to the counting-house for the key of the cellar, to get at the brass.
Q. Was it the business of one workman? - A. It was Cavernor's business to fetch it to the customers; he was the labourer.
Q. Did you afterwards search the cinders to see if the brass was gone?
Tilworth. I did, my Lord; they were then flat again.
SAMUEL SINGLETON sworn.
I am a constable; I was sent for on the 12th of January, about seven o'clock; when I came up, the prisoner and the two witnesses were all together; he had been stopped before I came. This brass(producing it) was delivered to me; I have had it ever since.(The punches were produced in Court, and fitted to the marks in the brass).
Mr. Penton. It came from Bristol; they are broke before they come to us.
Q. Is there any thing about that brass by which you should know it, independent of the place in which it was found? - A. No.
Q. You had brass of that kind? - A. Yes.
Q. Had the prisoner access to it? - A. He used to come for the key to the counting-house whenever he wanted it; he used to have it once or twice a day; I think he had had it that day, but I cannot be positive.
Q. You did not miss it, I suppose, out of thecellar? - A. It is impossible; I might be robbed of a ton and not know it; I have frequently from half a ton to four tons in my house at once.
Q. What is the value of these two pieces? - A. I have valued them at 2s. 6d. but they are worth 6s.
Prisoner's defence. Mr. Penton sent for me about five o'clock, and gave me two-pence for a basket of sand; at the same time, he knew I never paid more than three-halfpence for it; I fetched it, and pitched it in the yard; I went to look for him to give him the odd halfpenny; and, as I was coming back, I went into the necessary, and kicked my foot against this brass, and I picked it up out of the dust; I never saw the brass before in my life; I put it under my apron, and the last evidence stopped me with it; he took one pig from me, and I gave him the other.
The prisoner called three witnesses, who gave him a good character.
GUILTY. (Aged 42.)
Tried by the London Jury, before Mr. Baron THOMPSON.
[Transportation. See summary.]

152. CHARLES PILKINGTON was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 15th of January, two pounds, and three quarters, of black pepper, value 4s. the property of Abraham Newman, and William Thwaites.
WILSON JACKSON sworn.
I am warehouseman to Messrs. Newman and Thwaites, grocers; I was informed, by William Rose, that the prisoner had stole some pepper; I went after him to the George Inn, in the Borough, and asked him what he had got in his apron? and he said, a little sugar; I told him I knew it was a falsity, he had got pepper; and then he owned it was pepper; we came along to Fish-street-hill, and then I took from him two pounds three quarters of pepper; and then I took him home to his master's house, and gave him in charge to the constable.
Prisoner. He says he took the pepper from me; I gave it to him, and told him I had bought it.
Jackson. He first said it was given him, and then he said he gave sixpence for it.
Prisoner. I said three shillings and sixpence.
WILLIAM ROSE sworn.
I am shopman to Messrs. Abraham Newman and William Thwaites; all that I know of it is, that the prisoner had that pepper, and that it is Mr. Thwaites's property; I had it in my hand before it went out in the bag that it is in now; the constable has got it.
THOMAS PERRY sworn.
I am a constable; last Friday I was sent for to take charge of the prisoner; I asked him where he got it from; he said, he bought it at a porter's pitching-block, near London-bridge; that he gave sixpence for it. Mr. Rose gave it to me, and charged the prisoner with stealing it out of the cellar; it has been in my custody ever since, (produces it).
Rose. This is the prosecutor's property; there is a hole at one corner of the bag, and it is tore.
Jury. Q. Had it that hole before it went out? - A. Yes, it had.
Jury. Q. Can you swear to the pepper? - A. Yes.
Q. By what means? - A. I compared it with what we have at home, and I am equailly certain to the pepper as to the bag; I had it in my hand in the bag for upwards of half an hour.
Jury. (To Perry.) Q. Did you write upon it? - A. No.
Prisoner. Wilson put that mark upon it after he took it from me.
Jury. (To Rose.) Q. How long did you see that bag before it was taken from the prisoner? - A. It might be half an hour before, in the cellar; there was a hole at the bottom of the bag, and I was afraid the pepper would be lost; and I turned the bag underneath, that it might not be lost.
WILLIAM THWAITES sworn.
Q. Look at that pepper; can you identify it? - A. I cannot; it was a confirmation of it, that when it was compared with my other pepper, it was like it; there are different qualities of pepper and different colours.
Q. Can you say any thing to the bag? - A. No; I know nothing of it; the prisoner had been with me about three weeks.
Prisoner's defence. My Lord, the cellar that this Mr. Rose says he saw that bag in, lies at the back of the shop, and there is ten or eleven men go backwards and forwards into it; Mr. Rose ordered me to take a hamper to the George Inn, in the Borough; I pitched it at the bulk at London-bridge, and a man came up with that bag of pepper, and asked me if I would buy it; I told him I did not want pepper; he asked me 3s. 9d.; I told him I had not so much; I would give him 3s. 6d. which I did, and I lapped it up in my apron; then I went on the George Inn with my hamper, and as I was coming away, I met Wilson; he said I had got sugar in my apron; No, Sir, says I, I have not, it is pepper; he said he met our Frank upon the Hill, and he was sure he gave it me; we went on, and, upon Fish-street Hill, he said, I must give him the pepper; I said, if he would give me what I gave for it, he should have it; he said, I was his prisoner, and I gave him the pepper out of my apron; when I came back, Mr. Thwaites said, I will have every one of you to the Compter. I am sure that neither Mr. Rose, nor any body in theshop, can say they ever saw me touch a halfpennyworth in the shop, or in the cellar.
Q. (To Rose.) Did you see him go down into the cellar? - A. I did myself.
Q. At what time? - A. A little past twelve.
NOT GUILTY.
Tried by the London Jury, before Mr. COMMON SERGEANT.

153. JOHN CHESHIRE was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 14th of January, a woollen cloth cloak, value 10s. the property of William Brown.
SARAH BROWN sworn.
I am the wife of William Brown; my husband is a stone-mason; he lives in West Smithfield: On the 14th of January, about a quarter before eleven in the forenoon, I came home with some potatoes from market, I went out again for some turnips; I pulled off my cloak, and hung it over a chair; I returned in two minutes; and when I went in at the door, I saw that young man with my cloak doubled up under his arm, coming out of my room; I directly laid hold of him, and asked him what he was going to do with it; he said he was only going to look at it, and I would not let him go, till he threw down the cloak; he knocked me down several times, but I never let him go till I got assistance.
Q. Did you know any thing of him? - A. No; I never saw him before that I know of.
- WYLLIE sworn.
I am a patrole belonging to St. Sepulchre's; I was sent for to take the prisoner into custody; he is a drover in Smithfield, (produces the cloak.)
Prosecutrix. This is my cloak, I am very certain of it.
The prisoner made no defence, but called Jonathan Crow, a foot-dealer, and Thomas Cox, a drover, who gave him a good character.
GUILTY. (Aged 16.)
Tried by the London Jury, before Mr. COMMON SERGEANT.

154. WILLIAM BURRAMS was indicted for feloniously stealing, on the 24th of December, a silver watch, value 1l. 1s. the property of John Fuller.(The prisoner being deaf and dumb, the Jury were sworn to try whether he stood mute of his own obstinacy. or by the visitation of God.
SARAH BURRAMS sworn.
I am the mother of the prisoner; he was born deaf and dumb.
Q. You can make him understand you by signs? - A. Yes; I asked him about the watch, and he told me he did not steal it.
Q. He can write? - A. He writes from a copy, but he never writes his name right.(The Jury found that he stood mute, by the visitation of God).(The prisoner's mother was sworn as an interpreter).
ROBERT BLASSON sworn.
I am a shopman to Mr. Fuller, a hardwareman, at Smithfield-bars: On the 24th of December, between nine and ten in the evening, the prisoner came into our shop and pointed to the window; I thought he wanted some buckles, I shewed him some, and he shook his head; he then pointed up higher to the watches, and I took down three or four watches and shewed him.
Q. Did you know him before? - A. No, I never saw him before; he opened some of the watches and then pointed to some more, and while I was turning about to get another, he ran away with a silver one; I ran under the counter as fast as I could, and cried out stop thief; I ran across Smithfield, and just before I came up to him, he chucked it down as hard as he could; it was a moonlight night, and I was very near him, and somebody picked it up, I don't know who, and gave it to me; I did not stop to pick it up, because I was determined to catch him; here is a mark in it where he threw it down; I delivered him to the constable, and he was taken before a Magistrate.
SAMUEL BLADEN sworn.
I am an extra watchman; I heard the cry of stop thief in Smithfield; the prisoner was taken before I came up, the prosecutor had hold of him, and gave me charge of him; I took him to the watch-house, and he was committed to the Compter, he wrote his name in the watch-house.
SAMUEL ROBERTS sworn.
The prisoner was brought to me to St. Sepulchre's watch-house, and was charged with stealing a watch; I understood that he was deaf and dumb; I said, how should I know his name, he immediately got up and wrote his name, (produces it); whether he heard me, or whether it was by the motions of my mouth, I cannot say.
GUILTY.
He was recommended to mercy by the Jury, on account of his infirmity.
Whipped and discharged.
Tried by the London Jury, before The LORD CHIEF BARON.
[trialAccount t17960113-98]
155. JOHN PROCTOR was indicted for manslaughter upon Henry Read .(The witnesses were all prisoners in the Fleet, and were called, but did not appear, though notice had been given to bring them up by Habeas Corpus, and therefore the prisoner was acquitted).
